## Full unit/lesson map

### Unit 1 — Identity (pp.4–13)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.4–5):** personality compound adjectives, fixed expressions and idiomatic phrases ("set great store by", "get a kick out of"); describing personalities; paraphrasing what you hear.
- **2A Speaking and Vocabulary (p.6):** personality idioms in a quiz format (a social butterfly, a mover and shaker, a dark horse, a smart cookie, thin-skinned, a go-getter).
- **3A Listening and Vocabulary (p.7):** a talk about genes and personality / personality (psychometric) testing; vocabulary of testing (hereditary, nurture, disposition, trait, proponents, oversimplification).
- **4A Grammar (pp.8–9):** **continuous and perfect tenses** — present/past/future continuous and perfect forms contrasted, taught through a habits/running dialogue (Khalil and Issa) and marathon texts.
- **5A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.10–11):** *Does language change how you see the world?* — summary: the popular claim that Inuit languages have hundreds of words for snow is presented as largely a myth; the article then covers grammatical gender research (Spanish vs German speakers describing a bridge) and how language may shape perception. Verbs for thinking and understanding (grasp, comprehend, etc.).
- **6A Writing and Vocabulary (pp.12–13):** **a blog post about the past** — model text is a childhood-memory blog about a teddy bear, Oscar, who unexpectedly turns up with two eyes after a clear-out; paragraph-ordering and sentence-transformation work.

### Unit 2 — On the move (pp.14–23)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.14–15):** verbs of movement (stagger, stumble, stride, bolt…), movement idioms ("drive somebody up the wall", "be plain sailing"), travel verbs (steer, sail, backpedal…).
- **2A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.16–17):** *Lost words on the metro* — a prose fiction extract: Malek, from the countryside, crosses Plaça de Catalunya in Barcelona, follows a metro route to Les Corts/the stadium, and squeezes onto a packed train; taught through **reference devices** (Active Reading).
- **3A Grammar (pp.18–19):** **inversion and cleft sentences** for emphasis, built on the "Speeding in the sand" Dubai desert text ("Never before had I experienced such intense heat").
- **4A Listening and Vocabulary (p.20):** conversations about **space tourism** (ticket costs, space junk, settlements); phrasal nouns.
- **5A Speaking (p.21):** considering and comparing alternatives (last-chance holidays, holidaying at home, group size); Antarctica discussion.
- **6A Writing and Vocabulary (pp.22–23):** **an email of complaint** — informal email to a friend vs formal complaint to a coach company side by side; adverb–adjective collocations (gravely mistaken, wildly inaccurate).
- **Life Skills (pp.24–25): How to make a good decision** — decision steps box, practised over Sultan's blog about a trip to **Chile** (Santiago, Atacama Desert, Moon Valley and Mars Valley, travelling with Adnan).

### Unit 3 — Hard sell (pp.26–35)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.26–27):** business vocabulary, compound nouns and phrasal verbs through three unusual businesses (one-of-a-kind furniture; a wedding-cake business born from an expensive price tag; a goat rental service).
- **2A Listening and Vocabulary (p.28):** experts discuss psychological techniques for persuading people; synonyms for *persuade* graded by force (cajole, coax, entice — neutral convince, sway, urge — strong coerce, incite, pressure); opinion/attitude adjectives (crafty, credulous, cunning, gullible, shrewd…).
- **3A Speaking (p.29):** **negotiating** — two recorded negotiations (including babysitting rates with Mrs Alra'i) and a Speaking box of bargaining phrases.
- **4A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.30–31):** *Behavioural Economics and your daily life* — summary: economics as the study of choices; why pros-and-cons lists mislead; procrastination; the **sunk-cost fallacy** demonstrated with a birthday/chocolate-cake study. Economics vocabulary (p.30).
- **5A Grammar (pp.32–33):** **the Passive** — all main forms, reasons for using it, passive in an aged-photo retirement-savings experiment text; pronunciation: weak forms/contractions and *be* in passives.
- **6A Writing and Vocabulary (pp.34–35):** **a for-and-against essay** — model essay "Buying local: good or bad?"; Active Writing: paraphrasing; shopping vocabulary (stock up, shop around, mooch around).

### Unit 4 — Tastes (pp.36–45)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.36–37):** adjectives to describe food (chewy, gooey, gristly, fluffy, greasy…), **partitives** (a pinch/drop/handful/sprig/dab of…); a food artist talks about his job.
- **2A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.38–39):** *What tickles your taste buds?* — summary: hunger pangs, temptation and comfort eating; why we snack between meals (boredom, stress, anger); Active Reading: identifying paraphrases.
- **3A Grammar (pp.40–41):** **advanced conditional structures and inversion in conditionals** — mixed conditionals, *supposing/provided/unless/without/but for*, inverted *Had … not been / Were … to / Should …*; texts: "Ducks lend a helping beak" (Chinese farmer Tang's ducks eat insects and weeds and fertilise his fields) and the greater honeyguide bird.
- **4A Speaking (p.42):** proposing solutions, giving reasons and justifications (school project discussion about food waste — a third of food produced is wasted).
- **5A Listening and Vocabulary (p.43):** Zeina's vlog about **table manners**; table-manners vocabulary and idioms (haul over the coals, bring something home to somebody…).
- **6A Writing and Vocabulary (pp.44–45):** **a restaurant review** — two reviews of the same fusion restaurant, "The Big Combo" (Asian spices vs American burgers/deep-fried chicken); nominalisation taught in the Writing box.
- **Life Skills (pp.46–47): How to think critically about food choices** — how manufacturers make junk food look healthy (tiny portions, profit margins); class debate on a junk-food tax.

### Unit 5 — Do your best (pp.48–57)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.48–49):** idioms related to studying, collocations with *attempt* and *effort*, verbs + dependent prepositions; study-tips forum posts (Khalil: stop multitasking; Aisha: work at a desk, not a sofa; Randa).
- **2A Grammar (pp.50–51):** **modal and related verbs** — ability, obligation, deduction, permission across present and past (must have, can't have, be forced to, be required to, manage to…), from Aisha, Muna and Rola's exam stories; pronunciation: sentence stress with modals.
- **3A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.52–53):** an article on **sports psychology** — summary: elite athletes need more than fitness; sports psychologists teach coping skills for anxiety and career stress (muscle relaxation, breathing, meditation) and support injured players' confidence and pain management. Sporting-success and motivation collocations.
- **4A Listening and Vocabulary (p.54):** radio phone-in — four people describe how they motivate themselves; prepositional phrases.
- **5A Speaking (p.55):** **buying time in interview situations** — real odd interview questions (the tree question, the elephant question); fall-rise intonation.
- **6A Writing and Vocabulary (pp.56–57):** **an essay** — model essay on the panel topic "Memorisation of facts and figures is useless for our generation"; participle clauses in the Watch Out! box.

### Back matter
- **Culture Spot (pp.58–59): The British and the seaside** — blog/listening on the heyday of UK seaside resorts after WWII, pleasure piers (first built in the 19th century to transfer passengers from boats), fish and chips in newspaper.
- **Literature Spot (pp.60–61): *Black Beauty* by Anna Sewell** — plot/content summary only: the extract is the young horse's first-person recollection of the large pleasant meadow with a pond where he was raised, and his mother's advice to grow up gentle and good, never to kick or bite, and to lift his feet well when trotting; a lecture extract gives author facts to complete.
- **Word Lists (pp.62–66)** per unit/lesson with phonetics; **Grammar Reference (pp.67–71)** restating each unit's grammar (continuous/perfect tenses; inversion and clefts; the Passive across tenses; conditionals 0–3 plus inverted and *were to* forms; modal and related verbs incl. likelihood verbs *be likely/bound to*); **Irregular Verbs (p.72)**; **Communication (pp.73–74)** Student A/B pages for the Unit 3 negotiation and Unit 5 interview role-plays.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine for the book
- **Grammar spine:** continuous & perfect tenses (U1) → inversion and cleft sentences (U2) → the Passive (U3) → advanced conditionals incl. conditional inversion (U4) → modal and related verbs (U5). This is revision-and-extension level work (B2+–C1 by the Teacher's Book GSE tags), not first presentation of basic tenses.
- **Vocabulary spine:** personality adjectives/idioms → movement/travel → business/persuasion/economics → food/partitives/table manners → study/sport/motivation. Each unit's words are recycled in the Workbook and listed with IPA in the Word Lists.
- **Skills spine:** reading strategies are named per unit (understanding reference devices; identifying paraphrases); writing genres: blog post, email of complaint, for-and-against essay, restaurant review, essay.

## Honest negatives
- **No track printed:** nothing in the book states an academic (or any) track; the record's track label is external metadata.
- **Series-final verdicts (Grade 12 is the ceiling of Jordan High Note in this library):** **Essay writing — YES, fully taught by G12:** three essay types with full models across the two semesters (for-and-against here, general essay here, opinion essay in Semester 2), plus article, proposal, report, letter to the editor, review, blog and complaint email. **Conditionals — YES, fully taught:** Semester 1 Unit 4 covers advanced/mixed forms and conditional inversion, on top of the 0–3 recap in the Grammar Reference. **Passive — YES, fully taught as a tense system** in Unit 3 (including passive continuous forms in the reference section), **but the causative (*have/get something done*) does not appear anywhere in the Grade 12 books** (full-text search: zero hits). **Reported speech — only in advanced dress:** Semester 2 Unit 6 teaches reporting verbs, verb patterns and impersonal reporting structures; the Grade 12 books contain **no basic reported-speech unit** (no backshift system re-taught) — the course builds on it as prior knowledge.
- Literature is thin: one 2-page *Black Beauty* extract plus a lecture; no Jordanian or Arabic literature in English in this book.
- The PDF carries no audio; all listening depends on external audio files keyed by track numbers.
- Final PDF page (p.78) is blank.

## Full unit/lesson map

The Workbook mirrors the Student's Book unit by unit. Each unit = B-lessons matching the A-lessons, a **Self-assessment** page (1–5 confidence scale against named Student's Book pages) and a two-page **Revision** (Vocabulary and Grammar + Use of English + an integrated reading/listening/writing task).

- **Unit 1 Identity (pp.4–11):** 1B fixed expressions and compound-adjective word formation; 3B listening/vocabulary practice + Active Pronunciation (stress in compound adjectives); 4B continuous and perfect tenses; Revision reading: three texts on the significance of **first impressions** (personal life, job interviews).
- **Unit 2 On the move (pp.12–19):** 1B movement verbs and idioms (a ferry trip to France post); 3B inversion and cleft sentences ("Happy Days!" driving-test text: "Not only did I pass, but I passed with flying colours!"); 4B phrasal nouns + intonation in cleft sentences; Revision listening: holiday conversations incl. **flygskam (flight shame)**.
- **Unit 3 Hard sell (pp.20–27):** 1B business vocabulary and compound nouns ("How to start your own business"); 2B persuasion synonyms + words that drop a syllable; 5B the Passive (reasons for using it; a shopping-mall/hyperreality text and a voice-shopping text — **revised in this edition**, see comparison); Revision reading: *On the hunt for customers* — centuries of buying and selling, and modern tracking-cookie consent.
- **Unit 4 Tastes (pp.28–35):** 1B food adjectives and partitives (restaurant mini-conversations); 3B advanced conditional structures (gap items revised in this edition — first-person steak/cook examples); 5B table-manners vocabulary + emphatic syllable stress; Revision Use of English: *What a taste!* — research into the sense of taste vs the other senses.
- **Unit 5 Do your best (pp.36–43):** 1B study idioms, attempt/effort collocations, dependent prepositions ("Where did I put that piece of information?"); 2B modal and related verbs (complaint vs possibility intonation); Use of English: *Managing exam-related anxiety*; 4B motivation vocabulary + pronunciation; Revision reading: life coaching for college students ("Anyone can become a life coach — unlike a therapist, no professional qualifications are required" line of argument).
- **Back matter (pp.44–49):** Phrasal Verbs list (p.44); Prepositions in phrases and after verbs (pp.44–45); Word Building — prefixes/suffixes (p.46); Pronunciation Table (p.47); **Revision Answer Key (pp.48–49)** — answers to Revision exercises only; the B-lesson answers live in the Teacher's Book.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine for the book
- Same spine as the Student's Book, in practice form: continuous/perfect tenses → inversion/clefts → Passive → advanced conditionals → modals. Every grammar point gets a B-lesson, revision items and Use of English transformations (key-word transformations, word formation, open cloze) of the kind used in school-leaving exams.
- Vocabulary is practised, not re-taught: fixed expressions, collocations, dependent prepositions, phrasal verbs/nouns, with the pp.44–46 reference lists as the book's own glossary spine.

## Honest negatives
- The Workbook contains **no answer key for its main B-lessons** — only the two Revision pages per unit are keyed (pp.48–49); all other answers are in the Teacher's Book.
- No track is printed in this book either; the second edition renames the publisher (adds "and Evaluation") without any change of series or authors.
- Differences from the first edition are real but small (three exercise spots revised, one gap-fill text re-set, answer-key correction, line numbers added to one reading) — a learner holding the 2025 first edition is not holding a different course.
- The extra PDF page over the first edition (52 vs 51) is a watermark-only trailing leaf, not extra content.

# Jordan — English Grade 12 Jordan High Note, Grade 12: Workbook, Semester 1 (First edition (trial), 2025) [file 30953] — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03 (anchors 18/18)

## COMPARISON VERDICT (the two Term 1 workbooks)

**Verdict: the SAME book in two different editions — not different books, not different tracks.** File 30953 is the **First edition (trial), 2025**, adopted for 2025/2026. The other Term 1 workbook file is the **Second edition (revised and updated), 2026**, adopted for 2026/2027. Same series, same authors, same unit skeleton, same pagination; the second edition replaces the front matter and revises a handful of exercises.

**Evidence:**
1. **Imprints differ on every edition marker.** 30953: © Pearson/York Press 2025 only; **ISBN 978-9923-41-947-2**; deposit (2025/4/1878); "First edition (trial): 2025 / 1446"; adoption for **2025/2026**; Board decision 37/2025. Second edition: dual © lines (First edition trial 2025 / Second edition revised and updated 2026); **ISBN 978-9923-66-060-7**; deposit (2026/1/404); "Printed 2025, Reprinted 2026"; adoption for **2026/2027**; publisher renamed to "The National Center for Curriculum Development **and Evaluation**" and cover committee retitled "Adaptation Committee in Jordan".
2. **Whole-book sampled diff (all 51 content pages compared word-by-word after removing the second edition's Arabic review watermark):** the Contents pages are identical (same units, lessons, page ranges); 44+ pages are word-identical apart from watermark artefacts and hyphenation; substantive differences are confined to: (a) **printed p.5** (Unit 1 word-formation text "Personalities don't change – or do they?") — the gap-fill text is re-set in the second edition with different gap numbering and cue words (first edition opens the task with *sensation (SENSE)*, *transformation (TRANSFORM)*, *tact (TACT)* in a different order); (b) **printed p.24** (Unit 3, Lesson 5B, Passive) — instruction changed from "There are **six** extra phrases" (first edition) to "There are **three** extra phrases" (second edition), with the phrase bank and item set reworked; (c) **printed p.30** (Unit 4, Lesson 3B, advanced conditionals) — example items rewritten (first-person *I'd spent more on the steak…* / *If I were a more experienced cook…* replace the first edition's *we*-forms and past-perfect cook example, with matching answer options); (d) one item reworded on printed p.31; (e) **Revision Answer Key correction** on printed p.48 (an answer's verb form corrected, *'ve* → *'d* class); (f) the second edition adds printed line numbers (5–90) to the Unit 3 Revision reading; (g) one dropped word in a Unit 1 Revision item in the second edition ("mover and shaker" loses "and" in extraction — minor).
3. **Physical difference:** 51 PDF pages / 2,058,913 bytes (30953) vs 52 PDF pages / 6,453,877 bytes (second edition) — the second edition's extra page is a watermark-only trailing leaf, and its larger size comes from the watermark/image layer, not from added content.

**Filing consequence:** both files are genuine and both belong in the library; the second edition supersedes this one for course building, but nothing in this first edition is a different book, a different track, or a fake.

## Full unit/lesson map

- **Introduction (printed pp.4–19):** What is Jordan High Note?; the methodology (research-based, learner needs); key concepts — 21st-century education, cultural awareness, mediation activities, independence, Can-do statements at the end of every lesson, unit objectives up front; course components (with reduced Student's Book/Workbook page reproductions); a **unit walkthrough** (six lessons per unit: Lesson 1 always Vocabulary and Speaking, Lesson 6 always Writing; grammar in one dedicated lesson per unit; how Vocabulary, Reading, Speaking, Revision, Life Skills, Culture Spot and Literature Spot work); **Teaching pathways** (customising to context/level/class size); **How to teach for exams** (exam vs non-exam students; self-study resources); **How to flip the classroom**.
- **Teacher's Notes (printed pp.20–105):** Unit 1 Identity (pp.20–35) · Unit 2 On the move (pp.36–53) + Life Skills 01–02 (pp.54–55) · Unit 3 Hard sell (pp.56–71) · Unit 4 Tastes (pp.72–87) + Life Skills 03–04 (pp.88–89) · Unit 5 Do your best (pp.90–105). Each unit opens with its Mapping page, then notes in Student's Book order with the corresponding Workbook B-lesson and Revision notes interleaved; every listening's audioscript is printed inside its lesson notes (e.g. the Unit 1 personality-test talk, the Unit 3 goat-rental interview where over a hundred goats broke out of a garden, the Unit 5 interview audioscript 5.13 with Lucy and Jack on studying English Literature).
- **Culture Spot notes (pp.106–107)** and **Literature Spot notes (pp.108–109)** — mapping pages plus lesson notes and scripts for the British seaside lesson and the *Black Beauty* lesson (the Literature Spot notes record, among the lecture facts, that *Black Beauty* was not published in the USA until 1890 and that two million copies were in use within two years of release there).
- **Culture Notes (pp.110–111):** background notes per lesson (psychometric tests in job interviews; Plaça de Catalunya; Santiago de Chile — founded 1541, capital from 1818, population approx. 7 million, Spanish colonial architecture; and more).
- **Class Debates (pp.112–113):** teacher's notes (a five-step, 15-minute debate format) and a **Debating: useful language** page (presenting the motion, expressing certainty, etc.).
- **Photocopiable Resources (contents p.115; teacher's notes pp.116–121; worksheets printed pp.122–139):** 18 resources, one or two per key lesson — 1 Your other half (compound adjectives) · 2 Personality testing (audioscript gap-fill) · 3 It's functional (continuous/perfect tenses sentence cards) · 4 What have I done? (movement idioms) · 5 The scariest flight (reference devices) · 6 Under no circumstances … (inversion/clefts) · 7 Space tourism · 8 A head for business · 9 The art of persuasion · 10 Jordan's resources (the Passive, built on photos of Jordanian natural-resource sites) · 11 A fine example (food adjectives) · 12 Are you ready to cook? Go! (paraphrases, TV cooking competitions) · 13 Say it like this (conditionals) · 14 What if? (conditional inversion) · 15 Table manners · 16 Time for a pep talk · 17 You've got to think of a sentence! (modal and related verbs) · 18 Self-motivation.
- **Graphic Organisers (pp.141–149):** teacher's notes plus five organisers, one per Semester 1 writing genre — 1 A blog post · 2 An email (of complaint) · 3 A for-and-against essay · 4 A review · 5 An essay.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine for the book
- The guide teaches the teacher to teach the Student's Book spine (continuous/perfect tenses → inversion/clefts → Passive → advanced conditionals → modals) and adds what the student books never state: the **CEFR/GSE calibration** (the five-level Jordan High Note runs A2–C1 / GSE 30–85; Grade 12 objectives are tagged B2 to C1, individual ones to GSE 79–82), the curriculum **Theme** mapping for each unit (Unit 1 Theme 1 Society; Unit 2 Themes 6; Unit 3 Themes 2/6; Unit 4 Themes 2/5; Unit 5 Theme 1 — as printed on the mapping pages), and the exam-technique rationale (Use of English task types, flipped-classroom and self-study routes).

## Honest negatives
- The per-unit **Language Tests** the notes keep pointing to ("Assessment: Unit N Language Test") are **not included** in this book — they are a separate component not in the library.
- No audio files are included; the book prints scripts only.
- No track is printed; the guide also never names a Grade 12-specific CEFR level for the whole book — level claims exist only per-objective (GSE tags).
- One classification inconsistency to note for the record: the Teacher's Book is classed 371.3 while its own student books are classed 375.001 — a cataloguing difference in the books themselves, not an error in this filing.

## Full unit/lesson map

### Unit 6 — Feels good (pp.4–13)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.4–5):** body language — body-related collocations and idioms (fold your arms, purse your lips, stroke your chin…); Active Vocabulary: collocations.
- **2A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.6–7):** *Put On a Brave Face! Or … Should We?* — summary: an article on whether hiding stress and fear helps or harms; showing fear can intensify it, and experts discuss when expressing emotions is healthier than masking them. Emotion vocabulary (anticipation, apprehension, awe, contempt, loathing, rage, remorse, serenity).
- **3A Grammar (pp.8–9):** **reporting verbs and verb patterns, and impersonal reporting structures** — built around a podcast interview with the author of *The Power of Laughter* (why laughter is contagious); formalising sentences with *It is said/believed that…* and passive reporting patterns.
- **4A Listening and Vocabulary (p.10):** a podcast about travel to the **Dead Sea**; health vocabulary (exposure, infection, mineral, nourish, thrive), adverb + verb collocations.
- **5A Speaking (p.11):** evaluating ideas — a discussion about whether stress is best talked about or not, across age groups; falling intonation when expressing reservations.
- **6A Writing and Vocabulary (pp.12–13):** **an article** — model article on **forest bathing**: leave phones behind, immerse yourself in natural surroundings; article-writing advice box.
- **Life Skills (pp.14–15): Developing resilience** — text *Against the odds*: summary — Maha Al-Bargouthi (born 1962, Amman) had polio at birth which led to paraplegia, yet became a wheelchair athlete (200-metre wheelchair run) and Paralympic competitor; resilience discussion tasks.

### Unit 7 — The creative urge (pp.16–25)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.16–17):** vague language, fashion-related adjectives, materials and idioms (burgundy, cashmere, corduroy, clashing, flared, garish…); what to wear for a wedding, an Aqaba beach holiday, relaxing at home.
- **2A Grammar (pp.18–19):** **relative clauses; prepositional relative phrases; nominal relative clauses** — a lecture on Constable's *The Cornfield*; a gap-fill article about the Jordanian artist **Mohanna Durra**.
- **3A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.20–21):** the most loved and most hated words in English (dollop, kerfuffle, pulp, scissors, slurp, velvety, vivacious) and the set poem **"Pretty Words" by Elinor Morton Wylie** — content summary: the poet treats favourite words as pets — smooth words like enamelled fish, tender words like downy birds, shy dappled words like deer — a lyric about loving the sound and feel of words; poetic devices and metaphor work (Active Reading: understanding metaphors).
- **4A Listening and Vocabulary (p.22):** conversations about works of art/installations; adjectives to describe art (appealing, avant-garde, gimmicky, jaw-dropping, mediocre, overrated, soothing, tongue-in-cheek).
- **5A Speaking and Vocabulary (p.23):** describing a film — film genres (biopic, courtroom drama, disaster movie, period drama, post-apocalyptic, 3D/CGI and traditional animation…); a podcast review discussion.
- **6A Writing (pp.24–25):** **a letter to the editor** — model letter responding to an article about private funding of the arts; Active Writing: **hedging**.

### Unit 8 — Follow the crowd? (pp.26–35)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.26–27):** vocabulary of influence, creation and development; phrasal verbs with *come, hold, set, take*; a news report about pioneers.
- **2A Listening and Vocabulary (p.28):** a radio programme about how clothing has changed throughout history; adjective–noun collocations for clothing and the clothing industry; a mountain-expedition text.
- **3A Speaking (p.29):** Austin and Henry discuss their sixth-form college dress code; **hyperbole and understatement** as speaking devices.
- **4A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.30–31):** two linked readings — *Is there such a thing as having too many friends?* (summary: types of friends, socialising boosting mood, the limits of large friendship circles) and *What's wrong with being a loner?* (summary: introversion defended; being sociable is pressured but solitude can be healthy). Lifestyle vocabulary (a loner, a social butterfly, a team player, antisocial…).
- **5A Grammar (pp.32–33):** **articles** — a/an, the and zero article, including institutional zero article and fixed phrases with *the*; a "Why do we follow the crowd?" text.
- **6A Writing (pp.34–35):** **an opinion essay** — model essay "Traditional or modern values?"; Active Writing: using a formal register in essays.
- **Life Skills (pp.36–37): How to be a good team member** — two school social-action projects (Food for Thought desserts for Ramadan in Irbid; Art for Charity) and a group project-planning task.

### Unit 9 — Unbelievable (pp.38–47)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.38–39):** expressions and adjectives for illusion, surprise and incredulity (baffling, mesmerising, mind-boggling, mystifying, riveting); Active Vocabulary: word families.
- **2A Grammar (pp.40–41):** **uses of *will*; *will* vs *would*** — certainty, deduction, habits, irritation; a "What's the method?" article about method acting; pronunciation: word stress turning a fact into an expression of irritation.
- **3A Speaking (p.42):** making speculations about the past, present and future; hyperphantasia (extreme mental imagery) as the discussion opener.
- **4A Listening and Vocabulary (p.43):** a radio programme with sleep expert Professor Wilson about dreams; sleep/dream collocations.
- **5A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.44–45):** *Solving the Mystery of Nature's Illusions* — a comments page reacting to a documentary on natural optical illusions, including **the Wave** rock formation; collocations with *raise*, commenting vocabulary (intriguing, far-fetched, compelling…).
- **6A Writing (pp.46–47):** **a proposal** — model proposal for expanding a school photography club, written for the headteacher (current situation, why expansion is needed, what could be done).

### Unit 10 — Up the ladder (pp.48–57)
- **1A Vocabulary and Speaking (pp.48–49):** work-life balance idioms and collocations (constant connectivity, draw a line, strike a balance…); Active Vocabulary: register.
- **2A Grammar (pp.50–51):** **gerunds and infinitives** — verb patterns, forms and meaning changes (regret/remember/go on…), through the text *Fadia's footwear* about a successful businesswoman; a speaker's own success story.
- **3A Reading and Vocabulary (pp.52–53):** *Dreams shape your future* — summary: children's career dreams copy their parents, then change with age and stage; one new fantasy career bucks the trend; a chart of top career aspirations. Career-dreams collocations and synonyms.
- **4A Listening and Vocabulary (p.54):** a radio interview with a manager about leadership qualities (book, buck, cut, fuse, lap, rut, seat, stead idioms — blow a fuse, stuck in a rut, cut out to be…).
- **5A Speaking and Vocabulary (p.55):** **toning language down** — Sami discusses his job with Omar; expressing opinions about people and work without mincing (or while mincing) words.
- **6A Writing (pp.56–57):** **a report** — model "Report on work experience at Petra Primary School" (teaching-assistant placement); report structure in the Writing box.
- **Life Skills (pp.58–59): How to develop leadership skills** — two blog posts (an "accidental leader" whose history class trip made her a leader) and educational consultant Mariam on leadership.

### Back matter
- **Culture Spot (pp.60–61): Humour around the world** — what different cultures find funny (Latin American physical comedy and exaggerated gestures; everyday-event humour; deadpan, irony and sarcasm defined).
- **Literature Spot (pp.62–63): *Great Expectations* by Charles Dickens** — content summary only: in the extract, the boy narrator is brought to a decaying house; Uncle Pumblechook is not invited in and is left at the gate; a girl leads the boy through an unkempt courtyard and dark hall, up stairs lit by candlelight, to a dressing-room door and tells him to enter. A Fact Box and a student discussion recording support the reading.
- **Word Lists (pp.64–68)**; **Grammar Reference (pp.69–73)** — reporting verbs and impersonal reporting structures; relative and nominal relative clauses; articles; uses of *will/would*; gerunds and infinitives; **Irregular Verbs (p.74)**; **Communication (pp.75–76)** Student A/B pages for the Unit 9 speculation and Unit 10 toning-down role-plays.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine for the book
- **Grammar spine:** reporting verbs + impersonal reporting structures (U6) → relative clauses incl. prepositional and nominal relatives (U7) → articles (U8) → *will/would* uses (U9) → gerunds and infinitives (U10). Together with Semester 1 this completes the Grade 12 grammar ceiling: tense system, inversion/clefts, passive, advanced conditionals, modals, reporting, relatives, articles, *will/would*, gerunds/infinitives.
- **Vocabulary spine:** body language/emotions/health → fashion/vague language/art/film → influence/phrasal verbs/lifestyles/values → illusion/dreams → work-life balance/careers/leadership.
- **Writing spine (year total):** blog post, email of complaint, for-and-against essay, restaurant review, essay (Semester 1); article, letter to the editor, opinion essay, proposal, report (Semester 2) — ten taught genres, each with a full model.

## Honest negatives
- **Series-final verdicts (this book is the top of the course):** **Essay writing — YES, fully taught** (three essay types across the year with models and formal-register training). **Conditionals — YES, fully taught** in Semester 1 Unit 4 (advanced + inverted forms). **Passive — YES as a tense system** (Semester 1 Unit 3 + reference), **but no causative *have/get something done* anywhere in Grade 12**. **Reported speech — NOT taught as a basic system in Grade 12:** Unit 6 is reporting verbs, verb patterns and impersonal structures ("It is said that…", "He is believed to…"); the words "reported speech" appear only in passing (twice in this book) and there is no backshift presentation — the course treats it as already known.
- **No track printed** in the book; no CEFR level printed on the cover (the Teacher's Book frames the five-level course as A2–C1 and tags individual objectives up to GSE 82/C1).
- The PDF's last four pages (PDF pp.80–83) are near-blank watermark leaves, and a review-copy watermark sits across the content pages.
- Literature remains excerpt-only: one Dickens extract and one short poem (Wylie) are the whole set-literature offer of this semester.

## Full unit/lesson map

Same architecture as Semester 1: B-lessons per unit, a Self-assessment page, and a two-page Revision with Use of English work.

- **Unit 6 Feels good (pp.4–11):** 1B body-related collocations and idioms (sentence matching; a *Coffee and people-watching* gap text); 3B **reporting verbs and verb patterns + impersonal reporting structures** (rewrite tasks; a Use of English email about a comedy project); 4B listening (expert on an animal-documentary series — how humans communicate with animals) + stress in collocations; Revision: vocabulary/grammar, Use of English key-word transformations, listening interview with a writer about her book on reducing student stress (Swedish studies, breathing exercises, time-management lessons).
- **Unit 7 The creative urge (pp.12–19):** 1B clothing adjectives, vague language, clothing idioms (a crossword; second-hand/brand-new gap sentences); 2B **relative clauses, prepositional relative phrases, nominal relative clauses** (error-spotting, reduced relatives, *whatever/whichever/wherever/whoever/whenever* — a "Sisterhood" text); 4B listening (conversations about art) + word-stress pairs (ambiguity–ambiguous, atmosphere–atmospheric); Revision reading: *The Many Aspects of Creativity* (four texts: using employees' creativity, genius vs everyday creativity…); Revision Use of English: *Sustainable clothing*.
- **Unit 8 Follow the crowd? (pp.20–27):** 1B influence/creation/development vocabulary and phrasal verbs (word formation: originate→origin, construct→construction, credit→accreditation); 2B listening: a lecture on the history of fitness training (balancing, wrestling, crawling…) + stress in phrasal/prepositional verbs; 5B **articles** (a/an/the/ø, incl. *the Barkers*, *the poor* phrases); Revision text on **millennials**; Revision writing: notes-to-essay task on setting classroom rules.
- **Unit 9 Unbelievable (pp.28–35):** 1B adjectives/expressions of disbelief and surprise, word families (error correction; a dreams conversation word-formation task); 2B **uses of *will*, *will* vs *would*** (matching uses; replacing underlined parts with *would*); 4B listening: five speakers on memory + sounds dropped in fast speech; Revision Use of English: *Can you believe your eyes?* — the French term *trompe l'oeil* and visual trickery.
- **Unit 10 Up the ladder (pp.36–43):** 1B work-life balance collocations and idioms (strike the balance; error correction — "sign it a day" for *call it a day*); 2B **gerunds and infinitives** (*Baking your way to success* text; passive gerund/infinitive forms); 4B listening: an interview about working from home (distraction, pre-schoolers, self-discipline); Revision listening: five people on job interviews (two-task format); Revision speaking: comparing photos of work situations.
- **Back matter (pp.44–49):** Phrasal Verbs (p.44); Prepositions in phrases and after verbs (pp.44–45); Word Building (p.46); Pronunciation Table (p.47); Revision Answer Key (pp.48–49) — Revision exercises only.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine for the book
- Mirrors Student's Book Semester 2: reporting verbs/impersonal structures → relative clauses → articles → *will/would* → gerunds and infinitives, each in B-lesson + Revision + Use of English form (open cloze, key-word transformation, word formation, multiple choice) — the exam-practice layer of the course.
- Vocabulary practice: body idioms, fashion/vague language, phrasal verbs with *come/hold/set/take*, illusion/dream word families, work-life idioms; reference lists on pp.44–46 close the book's spine.

## Honest negatives
- As in Semester 1, **no answers for the main B-lessons** — only Revision pages are keyed in-book; the rest is in the Teacher's Book.
- No track printed; the review-copy watermark sits over the content pages of this PDF (legibility is still full via the text layer).
- The final PDF page is a near-blank watermark leaf (printed folio 50 only) — not missing content: the book's content genuinely ends at printed p.49 with the answer key.
